[music] So, what they thought was a $1,200 LTV is actually closer to 300 bucks. That's why gyms lock you into annual contracts. They're forcing the number higher. This is the reason for all of those free perks that companies offer. Loyalty cards, rewards, points, personalized discounts. It's all designed to extend LTV. The longer you stay, the more they can afford to spend chasing the next customer. And yes, okay, this is why subscriptions are deliberately annoying to cancel. There's an entire business model that banks on you forgetting to cancel. The longer you forget, the higher your lifetime value. So, here's the recap. Okay, LTV shows you how much a customer is worth. [music] CAC tells you how much you need to spend to get them. The ratio needs to be at least 3:1. And that brings us to the last piece of this holy trinity because so far we haven't talked about the actual product yet. And this is where the final metric comes into play, margins. Gross margin, that's the revenue minus the direct cost of producing the product or service. And net margin, revenue minus all expenses, production, marketing, salaries, rent, taxes, debt, everything. So your gross margin tells you how profitable your product is. Net margin tells you how profitable your business is. [music] So here's the math. Gross margin equals revenue minus the cost of goods sold divided by revenue. Net margin is net income divided by revenue. If you sell something for $100 and it costs $40 to produce, your gross margin is 60%. If after paying staff, rent, marketing, and taxes, you only keep $10, your net margin is 10%. Imagine you're selling cookies. Okay? You buy flour, sugar, and butter for $2, and you sell the cookie for $5. On paper, you made $3.
